### house communities
<a id="house-communities"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Residential groups in GDR apartment buildings, used for organisation and social control.
- **Explanation:** Hausgemeinschaften: tenants of a building organised for maintenance, festivities and political mobilisation; they also allowed neighbours to keep watch on one another.
- **German:** Hausgemeinschaften

### housewife marriage
<a id="housewife-marriage"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1900–1977
- **Short:** Legal model in which the wife ran the household and could work only if family duties allowed.
- **Explanation:** Family model written into West German civil law (Hausfrauenehe). Even the 1957 Equality Act let wives work only 'insofar as this is compatible with duties in marriage and family'. The 1977 marriage-law reform abolished it.
- **German:** Hausfrauenehe
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)

### Hufe
<a id="hufe"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** A peasant farm holding, often about 10–15 hectares, in the early medieval manor.
- **Explanation:** The standard farm on which a dependent peasant household lived and from which it owed dues and labour to the lord.
- **German:** Hufe

### Huguenots
<a id="huguenots"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** French Protestants who fled persecution; many settled in Brandenburg-Prussia after 1685.
- **Explanation:** Invited by the Edict of Potsdam, they made up about 20% of Berlin's population around 1700.
- **German:** Hugenotten
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)

<a id="huldigung-an-kaiser-wilhelm-i"></a>

- **Kind:** term · other
- **Dates:** 1871
- **Also written as:** Homage to Emperor Wilhelm I
- **Short:** Paul Bürde's 1871 picture of the celebration of the new German Emperor.
- **Explanation:** The image celebrates Wilhelm I's proclamation as German Emperor in 1871 — an Empire founded by princes and generals and proclaimed at Versailles, not voted by a parliament.
- **German:** Huldigung an Kaiser Wilhelm I.

### human dignity
<a id="human-dignity"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** since 1949
- **Also written as:** Human dignity · Art. 1
- **Ambiguous forms:** Art. 1 (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** The first principle of the Basic Law (Article 1): 'Human dignity shall be inviolable.'
- **Explanation:** Placed first as a lesson from Weimar and Nazism: all state power must respect and protect it, and the basic rights that follow bind the state directly and can be enforced in court. The 'eternity clause' makes it unamendable.
- **German:** Menschenwürde (Art. 1 GG)
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)

### humanism
<a id="humanism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** 15th–16th centuries
- **Also written as:** humanists · humanist
- **Short:** Renaissance scholarly movement reviving ancient learning and returning to original texts.
- **Explanation:** One of the pressures behind the Reformation: humanist scholars' study of the Bible in its original languages encouraged criticism of the Church.
- **German:** Humanismus
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)

### hunger crisis of 1846–47
<a id="hunger-crisis-1846-47"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1846–1847
- **Also written as:** hunger crisis · 1846–47 crisis
- **Short:** Harvest failures and famine in 1846–47 that deepened mass poverty before 1848.
- **Explanation:** Bad harvests caused famine, soaring food prices and food riots, on top of the mass poverty (Pauperismus) of the 1840s. Emigration surged. The crisis helped create the unrest that broke out in the revolutions of March 1848.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md`](../nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md)

### Hunger Plan
<a id="hunger-plan"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Short:** Nazi plan to starve 'tens of millions' in the Soviet Union to feed Germany and its army.
- **Explanation:** It shaped occupation policy and contributed to the deaths of millions, including Soviet POWs.
- **German:** Hungerplan
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)

- **Basis:** KB+K

### Hunger winter
<a id="hunger-winter"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1946–1947
- **Also written as:** hunger winter
- **Short:** The severe winter of 1946–47 with food shortages and cold.
- **Explanation:** Food shortages and extreme cold in 1946–47 left many Germans starving and freezing; rations in the Western zones fell as low as 1,000–1,500 calories a day at times, and people turned to black markets and barter.
- **German:** Hungerwinter
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`](../twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Huns
<a id="huns"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Nomadic people from the east whose arrival in the late 4th century put pressure on Europe.
- **Explanation:** Their movement westward in the late 4th century was one of the pressures that pushed Germanic-speaking groups into and against the Roman Empire.
- **Main chapter:** [`ancient/01_germanic_societies.md`](../ancient/01_germanic_societies.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Hussite wars
<a id="hussite-wars"></a>

- **Kind:** term · war/battle
- **Dates:** 1419–1434
- **Short:** Religious wars (1419–1434) following the execution of Bohemian reformer Jan Hus.
- **Explanation:** They were one of the 15th-century pressures behind demands for peace and law that led to the 1495 imperial reform.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Hut tax
<a id="hut-tax"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** head/hut taxes
- **Short:** Cash taxes on colonised households meant to force them into wage labour.
- **Explanation:** Payable only in cash, they compelled Africans to work for Europeans.
- **German:** Hüttensteuer
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### hyperinflation
<a id="hyperinflation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** 1923
- **Short:** Extremely rapid inflation; in 1923 a loaf of bread cost about 200 billion marks.
- **Explanation:** It peaked during the French-Belgian occupation of the Ruhr, when passive resistance was financed by printing money. Debtors, including industry and the state, gained; savers, creditors and pensioners were ruined. It was ended by the Rentenmark.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

## I

### Ideas of 1914
<a id="ideas-of-1914"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** 1914
- **Short:** Wartime claim that German 'culture' and community were superior to Western 'civilisation'.
- **Explanation:** Intellectuals contrasted German duty and community with Western individualism and commerce — a theme later used against the democratic republic.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/militarism.md` · `themes/nationalism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### identity control
<a id="identity-control"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Constitutional Court's claim to check EU acts against Germany's constitutional core.
- **Explanation:** The Court holds that some core principles of the Basic Law cannot be transferred to the EU and reserves the right to review EU acts against them.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md`](../contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### IG Farben
<a id="ig-farben"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** Giant German chemical company that used Auschwitz prisoners as slave labour.
- **Explanation:** It built a plant at Monowitz. Its executives were tried in one of the US successor trials at Nuremberg.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/industrialization.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Immigration Act
<a id="immigration-act"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 2005
- **Short:** 2005 law in which Germany first officially acknowledged being an immigration country.
- **Explanation:** Although Germany had large immigration since the 1950s, it officially accepted the fact only around 2000–2005, marked by this law regulating immigration and integration.
- **German:** Zuwanderungsgesetz
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### immigration history
<a id="immigration-history"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Official category: people who immigrated since 1950, or whose parents both did.
- **Explanation:** Statistical category used by Destatis. In 2025, 21.8 million people — 26.3% of Germany's population — had an immigration history, with Poland, Turkey, Ukraine, Russia and Syria the top origins.
- **German:** Einwanderungsgeschichte
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `contemporary/03_modern_society.md` · `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### imperial aristocracy
<a id="imperial-aristocracy"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Small group of related noble families holding counties and abbeys across the Carolingian empire.
- **Explanation:** These families were the backbone of Carolingian rule, and their regional power later fed the rise of the dukes.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/01_carolingian_world.md`](../medieval/01_carolingian_world.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### imperial church system
<a id="imperial-church-system"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Reichskirchensystem · imperial church
- **Short:** Contested idea that Ottonian-Salian kings deliberately ruled through bishops they appointed.
- **Explanation:** Kings endowed bishops with counties and royal rights and used them as administrators. The older view (Santifaller) saw a deliberate 'Reichskirchensystem'; Timothy Reuter (1982) argued it was a common European practice over-systematised by historians.
- **German:** Reichskirchensystem
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: whether this was a coherent policy (Reuter vs Santifaller).

### Imperial Circles
<a id="imperial-circles"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1500–1806
- **Also written as:** Reichskreise
- **Short:** Ten regional groupings of the Empire's territories for peace-keeping, defence and coinage.
- **Explanation:** They worked best in fragmented regions like Swabia and Franconia, where many small rulers needed to cooperate.
- **German:** Reichskreise
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Also in:** `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### imperial cities
<a id="imperial-cities"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** to 1806
- **Also written as:** Imperial cities · imperial city · Reichsstädte
- **Short:** Self-governing towns subject only to the emperor; they formed a college in the Imperial Diet.
- **Explanation:** About 50 existed after 1648. They lost on average about 31% of their population in the Thirty Years' War, and most were absorbed into larger states in 1803.
- **German:** Reichsstädte
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md` · `medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md` · `nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Imperial Diet
<a id="imperial-diet"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** to 1806
- **Also written as:** Diet · Diets · Perpetual Diet · Reichstag
- **Ambiguous forms:** Diet · Diets · Reichstag (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Assembly of the Holy Roman Empire's rulers and cities; permanent at Regensburg from 1663.
- **Explanation:** It had three colleges — electors, princes and imperial cities — chaired by the Archbishop-Elector of Mainz. From 1663 it became a permanent congress of envoys. Not to be confused with the later German parliament, also called Reichstag.
- **German:** Reichstag
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### imperial knights
<a id="imperial-knights"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Dates:** to 1806
- **Also written as:** Imperial knights · imperial knightships
- **Short:** Lesser nobles who ruled tiny territories directly under the emperor, with no prince above them.
- **Explanation:** Their holdings could be just a few villages. They were mediatised (placed under larger states) under Napoleon, keeping some privileges.
- **German:** Reichsritter
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Imperial protection
<a id="imperial-protection"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1884
- **Also written as:** imperial protection
- **Short:** Bismarck's formula for placing merchant holdings under state rule as 'protectorates'.
- **Explanation:** It created colonies in 1884–85 without calling them colonies.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Imperial Reform
<a id="imperial-reform"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1495
- **Also written as:** Imperial reform · imperial reform
- **Short:** 1495 reforms at the Diet of Worms: public peace, a supreme court and imperial circles.
- **Explanation:** They banned feud (Perpetual Public Peace), created the Imperial Chamber Court and led to the Imperial Circles, while the Common Penny tax failed. They made the Empire a legal-political federation.
- **German:** Reichsreform
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `medieval/03_medieval_society.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Imperialism
<a id="imperialism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** imperialism · imperialist
- **Short:** Policy of extending power over other lands through colonies and influence.
- **Explanation:** German imperialism was driven more by nationalist lobbies and prestige than by economic need.
- **German:** Imperialismus
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md` · `themes/authoritarianism.md` · `themes/nationalism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Imposed constitution
<a id="imposed-constitution"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1848
- **Also written as:** oktroyierte
- **Short:** Constitution granted by a monarch rather than agreed with parliament.
- **Explanation:** Frederick William IV imposed ('oktroyiert') a Prussian constitution in December 1848, revised in 1850; it lasted until 1918.
- **German:** oktroyierte Verfassung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Indemnity
<a id="indemnity"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** indemnity
- **Short:** Payment imposed on a defeated state.
- **Explanation:** France paid 5 billion francs in 1871, fuelling the Gründerzeit boom.
- **German:** Kriegsentschädigung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Indemnity Act
<a id="indemnity-act"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1866
- **Short:** 1866 law retroactively legalising Bismarck's budgetless rule; it split the liberals.
- **Explanation:** The Prussian parliament approved it after victory over Austria. Liberals who accepted it formed the National Liberals.
- **German:** Indemnitätsgesetz
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Indirect taxes
<a id="indirect-taxes"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** indirect taxes
- **Short:** Taxes on goods and consumption rather than on income or property.
- **Explanation:** The Empire's central government relied on customs and indirect taxes; direct taxes belonged to the states until the 1913 defence levy.
- **German:** indirekte Steuern
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### indulgences
<a id="indulgences"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Church certificates reducing punishment for sins, sold for money; Luther attacked their sale in 1517.
- **Explanation:** In Catholic teaching an indulgence reduced the time a soul spent in purgatory. Luther's 1517 protest against their sale sparked the Reformation.
- **German:** Ablass
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Industrial Code
<a id="industrial-code"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1869
- **Short:** 1869 North German law allowing workers' coalitions and freedom of trade.
- **Explanation:** It legalised unions and strikes, previously banned in most states.
- **German:** Gewerbeordnung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Industrial courts
<a id="industrial-courts"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1890
- **Short:** Labour courts (1890) settling disputes between workers and employers.
- **Explanation:** Introduced in the 'New Course' after Bismarck's fall.
- **German:** Gewerbegerichte
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Industrialisation
<a id="industrialisation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** c. 1835–1914
- **Also written as:** industrialisation
- **Short:** Shift from farming and crafts to factory production.
- **Explanation:** Within two generations it made Germany Europe's leading industrial economy, creating a new working class and the 'social question'.
- **German:** Industrialisierung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md` · `themes/class.md` · `themes/industrialization.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### inflation
<a id="inflation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Short:** A general, sustained rise in prices, so that money buys less.
- **Explanation:** War debts, reparations and social demands pushed prices up after 1918. The 1923 inflation destroyed middle-class trust in the republic, although the Nazi breakthrough came later, in the Depression.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `03_MENTAL_MODEL.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `ancient/02_roman_frontier.md` · `contemporary/01_post_reunification.md` · `themes/class.md` · `themes/industrialization.md` · `twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### informational self-determination
<a id="informational-self-determination"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1983
- **Short:** The right to control one's own personal data, recognised by Germany's Constitutional Court in 1983.
- **Explanation:** Principle established in the Constitutional Court's 1983 ruling on a planned census: individuals have a basic right to decide what personal data the state collects and uses. It became the foundation of German data-protection law.
- **German:** informationelle Selbstbestimmung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### infringement procedure
<a id="infringement-procedure"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Also written as:** infringement procedures
- **Short:** Legal action by the European Commission against a member state that breaks EU law.
- **Explanation:** The main tool for enforcing EU law; it can end at the Court of Justice with fines.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md`](../contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### inner-German trade
<a id="inner-german-trade"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** Inner-German trade
- **Short:** Trade between the two German states, treated by West Germany as domestic.
- **Explanation:** Because the FRG did not count it as foreign trade, the GDR gained privileged access to the EEC market.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Innere Führung
<a id="innere-fuhrung"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1955–
- **Also written as:** citizen in uniform · citizens in uniform
- **Short:** Bundeswehr leadership principle: soldiers as 'citizens in uniform' with rights and conscience.
- **Explanation:** Adopted when West Germany rearmed in 1955, it placed the army under parliamentary control and rejected blind obedience — a deliberate break with the Prussian-German army's autonomy from parliament.
- **German:** Innere Führung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/militarism.md` · `themes/political_power.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Institute for Contemporary History
<a id="institute-for-contemporary-history"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** IfZ
- **Short:** Munich-based research institute that produced major studies of the Treuhand (2017–22).
- **Explanation:** A leading German historical research institute (IfZ). Its research project on the Treuhand concluded that the agency became a symbol of the decline in productivity and employment and of social upheaval in eastern Germany.
- **German:** Institut für Zeitgeschichte (IfZ)
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### intentionalism
<a id="intentionalism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Intentionalism
- **Short:** View that Nazi policy followed Hitler's long-held intentions.
- **Explanation:** Bracher, Hildebrand, Jäckel and Dawidowicz argued this; today's synthesis combines it with functionalism.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Also in:** `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md` · `twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: intentionalism vs functionalism.

### international relations (IR)
<a id="international-relations"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** IR
- **Short:** Academic discipline studying relations between states.
- **Explanation:** IR textbooks often date the modern system of sovereign states to 1648; historians dispute this.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md`](../early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Interparty Committee
<a id="interparty-committee"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1917
- **Short:** WWI committee of the Reichstag majority parties (SPD, Centre, Progressives).
- **Explanation:** It coordinated the parties behind the 1917 Peace Resolution and rehearsed the coalition that would govern the Weimar Republic.
- **German:** Interfraktioneller Ausschuss
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Interregnum
<a id="interregnum"></a>

- **Kind:** term · era/period
- **Dates:** 1250s–1273
- **Short:** Period (1250s–1273) without a generally recognised king in the Empire.
- **Explanation:** It ended with the election of Rudolf of Habsburg; the princes preferred weak kings, and the electoral college took shape in these decades.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Intershop
<a id="intershop"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1962–1990
- **Short:** GDR chain of shops selling Western goods only for hard currency.
- **Explanation:** Stores where East Germans with D-Marks (often from relatives in the West) could buy Western products, creating a two-tier society by the 1980s.
- **German:** Intershop
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### invasion of Poland
<a id="invasion-of-poland"></a>

- **Kind:** term · war/battle
- **Dates:** 1 September 1939
- **Also written as:** Invasion of Poland · invaded Poland
- **Short:** German attack on Poland on 1 September 1939 that began the Second World War in Europe.
- **Explanation:** Britain and France declared war two days later. In occupied Poland the regime began mass shootings and set up ghettos (1939–41), early stages of the persecution that led to the Holocaust.
- **German:** Überfall auf Polen
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### investiture
<a id="investiture"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Investiture · invested
- **Short:** The formal installation of someone in an office, especially a bishop.
- **Explanation:** Who could invest bishops caused the Investiture Controversy. After 1122, the Church conferred the spiritual office, the king the worldly rights.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `medieval/01_carolingian_world.md` · `medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Investiture Controversy
<a id="investiture-controversy"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1075–1122
- **Also written as:** Investiture Contest
- **Short:** Struggle (1075–1122) between popes and emperors over who could appoint bishops.
- **Explanation:** Pope Gregory VII challenged Henry IV's right to appoint bishops. The conflict (with Canossa, 1077) ended in the Concordat of Worms (1122); the king lost sacred control of the Church, and princes became kingmakers.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `medieval/01_carolingian_world.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Iron and blood
<a id="iron-and-blood"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1862
- **Also written as:** iron and blood
- **Short:** Bismarck's 1862 phrase: great questions decided by war, not speeches and majority votes.
- **Explanation:** Spoken to the Prussian parliament's budget committee, it came to stand for unification through war rather than parliamentary consent.
- **German:** Eisen und Blut
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Iron Kingdom
<a id="iron-kingdom"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Dates:** 2006
- **Short:** Christopher Clark's 2006 history of Prussia.
- **Explanation:** It portrays Prussia as fragile and improvised rather than inherently militarist.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Islam
<a id="islam"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** Muslims
- **Short:** The religion of Germany's roughly 5–6 million Muslims.
- **Explanation:** Largely brought to Germany by labour migration from Turkey and later by refugees. The place of Islam in German society is one of the open debates about integration and identity.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/03_modern_society.md`](../contemporary/03_modern_society.md)
- **Also in:** `contemporary/01_post_reunification.md` · `themes/minorities_and_migration.md` · `themes/nationalism.md` · `themes/religion.md`

### Italian unification
<a id="italian-unification"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1859–1861
- **Short:** Creation of an Italian nation-state through war and diplomacy.
- **Explanation:** It showed Germans how war could unify a nation.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### itinerant kingship
<a id="itinerant-kingship"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** itinerant court
- **Short:** Rule by a king who constantly travelled between estates and churches, having no capital.
- **Explanation:** Carolingian and later German kings travelled constantly to show themselves, judge, and be fed by royal estates and churches. It reflects personal, not bureaucratic, government.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `medieval/01_carolingian_world.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### itio in partes
<a id="itio-in-partes"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1648
- **Short:** Latin: 'going into parts' — splitting the Diet into Catholic and Protestant blocs on religious questions.
- **Explanation:** Neither side could outvote the other: religious matters needed agreement between the blocs.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md`](../early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### ius emigrandi
<a id="ius-emigrandi"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1555
- **Also written as:** right to emigrate · right of emigration
- **Short:** Latin: 'right to emigrate' — subjects who rejected their ruler's faith could leave.
- **Explanation:** Granted by the Peace of Augsburg in 1555, it is often seen as an early individual right: dissenters could not be forced to convert, but had to move away.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### ius soli
<a id="ius-soli"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 2000–
- **Short:** Citizenship by birth in a country's territory ('right of the soil').
- **Explanation:** Latin for 'right of the soil'. Germany long based citizenship on descent; the 2000 reform introduced partial ius soli for children of long-term residents, a shift toward civic nationhood.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `contemporary/03_modern_society.md` · `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

## J

### Jehovah's Witnesses
<a id="jehovah-s-witnesses"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Christian group persecuted for refusing oaths and military service.
- **Explanation:** A Christian group persecuted because members refused oaths and military service. Many were sent to camps; about 1,700 were killed.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jesuits
<a id="jesuits"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** founded 1540
- **Also written as:** Jesuit
- **Short:** Catholic religious order central to the Counter-Reformation, especially through schools.
- **Explanation:** The Society of Jesus spearheaded Catholic renewal after the Reformation.
- **German:** Jesuiten
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md` · `themes/education.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jew count
<a id="jew-count"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** October 1916
- **Also written as:** Judenzählung
- **Short:** WWI army census of Jewish soldiers, prompted by antisemitic accusations of shirking.
- **Explanation:** It deeply wounded German Jews, about 100,000 of whom served; its results were never published. Jewish soldiers in fact died at the same rate as non-Jews — a fact later denied by antisemites.
- **German:** Judenzählung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jew houses
<a id="jew-houses"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** Buildings into which Jews were forced to move from 1939, before deportation.
- **Explanation:** From 1939 Jews were forced out of their homes into designated buildings. This isolated them, helped the regime seize their flats and prepared their deportation from October 1941.
- **German:** Judenhäuser
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jewish edict (1812)
<a id="jewish-edict-1812"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1812
- **Also written as:** Jewish edict
- **Short:** Prussian law granting Jews citizenship with restrictions.
- **Explanation:** Part of the partial emancipation of the Napoleonic period, partly rolled back after 1815.
- **German:** Judenedikt
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Jewish emancipation
<a id="jewish-emancipation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** c. 1800–1871
- **Also written as:** emancipation · Emancipation
- **Ambiguous forms:** Emancipation · emancipation (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** The granting of equal civil rights to Jews, which was slow and uneven in Germany.
- **Explanation:** Partial rights granted in the Napoleonic era were rolled back in some places after 1815. The 1848 Basic Rights promised full equality; legal emancipation was completed by the North German Confederation (1869) and the Empire (1871).
- **German:** Judenemanzipation
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `early_modern/05_enlightenment.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Jewish question
<a id="jewish-question"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Antisemitic phrase presenting Jews as a 'problem' needing a 'solution'.
- **Explanation:** Nazi officials' competition on this issue drove radicalisation towards genocide.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jewish star
<a id="jewish-star"></a>

- **Kind:** term · other
- **Dates:** 1941
- **Short:** Yellow badge Jews in Germany had to wear from September 1941.
- **Explanation:** It marked Jews publicly in the weeks before deportations from Germany began.
- **German:** Judenstern
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jews
<a id="jews"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** Jewish
- **Short:** Religious and ethnic minority present in German lands since Roman times.
- **Explanation:** A 321 CE decree is the earliest evidence of Jews north of the Alps. Medieval communities (ShUM) flourished under royal or episcopal protection but suffered massacres in 1096, 1298, 1336–38 and 1348–50, and 15th-century expulsions.
- **Main chapter:** [`themes/minorities_and_migration.md`](../themes/minorities_and_migration.md)

### Joint Declaration
<a id="joint-declaration"></a>

- **Kind:** term · treaty/agreement
- **Dates:** 2021
- **Short:** Declaration initialled by Germany and Namibia in 2021 on the genocide.
- **Explanation:** Herero and Nama representatives dispute implementation.
- **German:** Gemeinsame Erklärung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)

### Joint-stock banks
<a id="joint-stock-banks"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** from 1850s
- **Also written as:** joint-stock banks
- **Short:** Banks owned by shareholders, financing industry.
- **Explanation:** Early examples include the Darmstädter Bank (1853).
- **German:** Aktienbanken
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Josephinism
<a id="josephinism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Short:** Joseph II's state-controlled reform of the Catholic Church.
- **Explanation:** It included dissolving contemplative monasteries and subordinating church affairs to the state.
- **German:** Josephinismus
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Journal of Early Medieval Northwestern Europe
<a id="journal-of-early-medieval-northwestern-europe"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Also written as:** JEMNE
- **Short:** Open-access academic journal (abbreviated JEMNE) on early medieval northwestern Europe.
- **Explanation:** It published Fruscione's article 'On “Germanic”', cited in the KB for treating 'Germanic' as a relational rather than an ethnic category.
- **Main chapter:** [`ancient/01_germanic_societies.md`](../ancient/01_germanic_societies.md)

### journeyman
<a id="journeyman"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** journeymen
- **Short:** Craftsman who had finished an apprenticeship but was not yet a master.
- **Explanation:** The middle step between apprentice and master.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Judeo-Bolshevism
<a id="judeo-bolshevism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Short:** Nazi propaganda myth that communism was a Jewish conspiracy.
- **Explanation:** Destroying 'Judeo-Bolshevism' was a stated aim of the invasion of the Soviet Union.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/authoritarianism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Jugendweihe
<a id="jugendweihe"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Dates:** 1954–
- **Short:** Secular coming-of-age ceremony that replaced church confirmation in the GDR.
- **Explanation:** State-promoted ceremony for 14-year-olds, introduced in 1954, pledging loyalty to socialism. It displaced Christian confirmation and remains popular in eastern Germany.
- **German:** Jugendweihe
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Junge Gemeinde
<a id="junge-gemeinde"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Short:** Protestant church youth groups persecuted by the GDR in 1952–53.
- **Explanation:** Church youth groups that the SED campaigned against as 'enemy' organisations, expelling members from schools, before easing off after June 1953.
- **German:** Junge Gemeinde
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Junkers
<a id="junkers"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** Junker
- **Short:** Landed nobility east of the Elbe, who ran estates and supplied Prussia's officers and officials.
- **Explanation:** They were masters of their peasants — landlord, police chief and judge — in exchange for serving the crown. Many were not rich. Whether they set Germany on a harmful 'special path' is debated.
- **German:** Junker
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)

- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: their role in a German Sonderweg.

### justification by faith alone
<a id="justification-by-faith"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Justification by faith alone
- **Short:** Luther's core teaching that people are saved by faith, not by good works or church rituals.
- **Explanation:** Together with 'scripture alone' it undermined the Church's system of sacraments and indulgences.
- **German:** sola fide
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

## K

### Kaiser
<a id="kaiser"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Dates:** 1871–1918
- **Also written as:** Emperor
- **Ambiguous forms:** Emperor (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** German Emperor; in 1871–1918 always the King of Prussia.
- **Explanation:** He appointed the Chancellor, commanded the armed forces and declared war with Bundesrat consent. His power of command over the army was outside parliamentary control.
- **German:** Kaiser
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `nineteenth_century/05_unification.md` · `nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md` · `themes/german_identity.md` · `themes/militarism.md` · `themes/nationalism.md` · `themes/political_power.md` · `twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md` · `twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kaiserreich
<a id="kaiserreich"></a>

- **Kind:** term · era/period
- **Dates:** 1871–1918
- **Also written as:** German Empire · Empire · kaiserreich
- **Ambiguous forms:** Empire (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** The German Empire of 1871–1918: a federal monarchy under the King of Prussia as Kaiser.
- **Explanation:** It combined universal male suffrage with a government answerable to the Kaiser, not parliament. Rapid industrialisation, mass parties, persecution of Catholics and socialists and colonial expansion marked it. Historians debate whether it was on a 'special path' or democratising.
- **German:** Kaiserreich
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)

- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: 'special path' vs democratising society.

### Kapp Putsch
<a id="kapp-putsch"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** March 1920
- **Short:** Failed right-wing coup attempt of 1920, defeated by a general strike.
- **Explanation:** Nationalist officers and politicians led by Wolfgang Kapp tried to overthrow the republic. The army largely refused to defend the government, but a general strike by workers and officials paralysed the country and the putsch collapsed within days.
- **German:** Kapp-Putsch
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Katholikentag
<a id="katholikentag"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** from 1848
- **Also written as:** Catholic Congress
- **Short:** German Catholic Congress, first held in Mainz in 1848.
- **Explanation:** A mass gathering of Catholic associations, part of the rise of political Catholicism; it is still held today.
- **German:** Katholikentag
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Khrushchev ultimatum
<a id="khrushchev-ultimatum"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1958
- **Short:** Soviet demand in 1958 that the Western powers leave West Berlin.
- **Explanation:** Nikita Khrushchev called for West Berlin to become a demilitarised 'free city', opening the Berlin crisis that ended with the building of the Wall in 1961.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kiel mutiny
<a id="kiel-mutiny"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1918
- **Short:** Revolt of German sailors at Kiel in late October–early November 1918.
- **Explanation:** Sailors refused a last naval sortie; the revolt spread across Germany as workers' and soldiers' councils and became the revolution that ended the monarchy.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### King in Prussia
<a id="king-in-prussia"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Dates:** 1701
- **Short:** Title taken by Frederick III of Brandenburg in 1701 for Prussia, which lay outside the Empire.
- **Explanation:** Prussia (the former duchy, later East Prussia) lay outside the Holy Roman Empire, so the Hohenzollerns could be kings there without challenging the emperor.
- **German:** König in Preußen
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### king of the Romans
<a id="king-of-the-romans"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Short:** Title of the elected German king before his imperial coronation.
- **Explanation:** Elected by the princes and crowned in Aachen, he became emperor through papal coronation in Rome (until the 16th century).
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### kingmaker
<a id="kingmaker"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** A small party whose choice of coalition partner decides who governs.
- **Explanation:** In West Germany this was the liberal FDP, which for decades held the balance between the two big parties and so determined whether the CDU/CSU or the SPD led the government.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kleindeutsch
<a id="kleindeutsch"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** small German · kleindeutsch
- **Short:** 'Small German' solution: a united Germany excluding Austria, under Prussian leadership.
- **Explanation:** The option chosen by the 1849 constitution and realised in 1871. Its opposite, the 'großdeutsch' (greater German) solution, would have included Austria.
- **German:** kleindeutsch
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)

### knights
<a id="knights"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Lower nobility of mounted warriors.
- **Explanation:** In Germany many knights descended from unfree administrators and warriors (ministeriales) who rose into the nobility in the 12th–13th centuries.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/03_medieval_society.md`](../medieval/03_medieval_society.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kohlrüben
<a id="kohlruben"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** German for swede turnips, the emergency staple food of the 1916/17 hunger winter.
- **Explanation:** Official leaflets told people how to cook them, as the blockade and poor harvests made other food scarce.
- **German:** Kohlrüben

### Kombinate
<a id="kombinate"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Dates:** 1979–1990
- **Short:** Large GDR industrial conglomerates grouping many state enterprises.
- **Explanation:** From 1979 GDR enterprises were grouped into giant combines (singular: Kombinat) managing whole branches of industry.
- **German:** Kombinate

### Kommandogewalt
<a id="kommandogewalt"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** power of command
- **Short:** The Kaiser's 'power of command' over the army, outside ministerial responsibility.
- **Explanation:** It kept the army outside parliamentary control, as the Zabern affair showed.
- **German:** Kommandogewalt
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)

### Kontore
<a id="kontore"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** Trading offices of the Hanse abroad.
- **Explanation:** The Hanse ran offices in London (the Steelyard), Bruges, Bergen and Novgorod, where its merchants lived and traded under shared privileges.
- **German:** Kontore
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)

### Kontribution
<a id="kontribution"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** Prussian land tax paid mainly by peasants; nobles in core provinces were largely exempt.
- **Explanation:** Together with domain income and town excise it financed the Prussian state; the burden fell on peasants, not nobles.
- **German:** Kontribution
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)

### Korean War
<a id="korean-war"></a>

- **Kind:** term · war/battle
- **Dates:** 1950–1953
- **Also written as:** Korean War boom
- **Short:** War in Korea whose demand for goods boosted West German exports in 1950–51.
- **Explanation:** Conflict between communist North Korea (backed by China and the USSR) and South Korea (backed by the US-led UN). Worldwide demand for industrial goods produced a 'Korean boom' that helped West Germany's economy take off.

### KPD
<a id="kpd"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1918–1956
- **Also written as:** Communists · Communist · Communist KPD
- **Short:** Communist Party of Germany, founded 1918/19, revolutionary and aligned with Moscow.
- **Explanation:** Hostile to the republic, from 1928 it attacked the SPD as 'social fascists', deepening the left's paralysis. It attracted many unemployed voters. After the Reichstag fire it was the first target of Nazi terror.
- **German:** Kommunistische Partei Deutschlands

### Kreisau Circle
<a id="kreisau-circle"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Short:** Resistance group planning a moral and political new order for post-Nazi Germany.
- **Explanation:** Named after Moltke's estate in Silesia; led by Moltke and Yorck, it had links to the 20 July plotters.
- **German:** Kreisauer Kreis
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kreuzzeitung
<a id="kreuzzeitung"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Dates:** 1848–1939
- **Short:** Conservative Prussian newspaper founded in 1848.
- **Explanation:** Its name came from the Iron Cross in its title. It helped organise conservatism against the revolution.
- **German:** Kreuzzeitung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Krieg der Illusionen
<a id="krieg-der-illusionen"></a>

- **Kind:** term · other
- **Dates:** 1969
- **Short:** Fritz Fischer's 1969 book ('War of Illusions') on German policy before 1914.
- **Explanation:** It extended his argument that Germany's leaders deliberately pursued war.
- **German:** Krieg der Illusionen
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kriegervereine
<a id="kriegervereine"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Short:** Veterans' associations with millions of members in the Empire.
- **Explanation:** They spread militarist and monarchist values.
- **German:** Kriegervereine
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Kriegstüchtigkeit
<a id="kriegstuchtigkeit"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** 'War-readiness': contested goal for Germany's armed forces and society debated since 2022.
- **Explanation:** The word marks a break with post-war military restraint after Russia's invasion of Ukraine; the KB places it with rearmament and the 2025 service law in a new phase of German military policy.
- **German:** Kriegstüchtigkeit
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md`](../contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Krupp
<a id="krupp"></a>

- **Kind:** term · other
- **Short:** Steel and arms company of Essen, symbol of German heavy industry.
- **Explanation:** A leading example of the industrial bourgeoisie. Krupp practised paternalist 'master in one's own house' management: company housing and pensions combined with hostility to unions.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kulturkampf
<a id="kulturkampf"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1871–1887
- **Short:** Bismarck's and the liberals' campaign against the Catholic Church in the 1870s.
- **Explanation:** Measures included the pulpit paragraph, expulsion of Jesuits, the May Laws and civil marriage. A quarter of Prussian Catholic parishes were vacant at its peak. It failed: Catholics rallied to the Centre Party.
- **German:** Kulturkampf
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md` · `themes/minorities_and_migration.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Kulturnation
<a id="kulturnation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** cultural nation · Cultural nation
- **Short:** 'Cultural nation': a nation defined by shared language, literature and history, not by a state.
- **Explanation:** Around 1800, with no German state, intellectuals such as Herder and the Romantics imagined Germany as such a cultural community.
- **German:** Kulturnation
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md`](../nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `early_modern/05_enlightenment.md` · `themes/german_identity.md` · `themes/nationalism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

## L

### Labour book
<a id="labour-book"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Dates:** 1935
- **Also written as:** Arbeitsbuch · labour book
- **Short:** Workbook introduced in 1935 recording a worker's jobs and controlling job changes.
- **Explanation:** It let the state steer labour and restrict workers' freedom to change employer.
- **German:** Arbeitsbuch
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Land reform
<a id="land-reform"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1945
- **Also written as:** land reform
- **Short:** 1945 expropriation of estates over 100 hectares in the Soviet zone.
- **Explanation:** In the Soviet zone, estates over 100 hectares were seized without compensation and redistributed. It destroyed the Junker class of landed nobles and was part of using denazification to carry out a social revolution.
- **German:** Bodenreform
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`](../twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `themes/class.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md` · `twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Länder
<a id="lander"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** Land
- **Ambiguous forms:** Land (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Germany's 16 federal states, each with its own constitution, government and parliament.
- **Explanation:** Plural of 'Land', the German states. They are not mere districts: they control education, police and culture, carry out most federal laws and take part in federal law-making through the Bundesrat.
- **German:** Länder (singular: Land)
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md`](../contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `contemporary/01_post_reunification.md` · `contemporary/03_modern_society.md` · `contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md` · `medieval/01_carolingian_world.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md` · `themes/education.md` · `themes/political_power.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md` · `twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md` · `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md` · `twentieth_century/12_reunification.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### landesherrliches Kirchenregiment
<a id="landesherrliches-kirchenregiment"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** landesherrliche Kirchenregiment
- **Short:** The prince's rule over the church in his territory — the Protestant ruler as church head.
- **Explanation:** After the Reformation, Protestant princes took over church government, appointments and property. The Diet of Speyer (1526) gave this development its first impulse.
- **German:** landesherrliches Kirchenregiment
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Landesherrschaft
<a id="landesherrschaft"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** territorial lordship · Territorial lordship
- **Short:** Territorial lordship: a prince's combined rule over a territory.
- **Explanation:** From the 13th century princes combined land, courts, tolls, mints, forests and fortresses into territories. This territorial power, not the emperor, shaped daily government.
- **German:** Landesherrschaft
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/03_medieval_society.md`](../medieval/03_medieval_society.md)
- **Also in:** `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Landeshoheit
<a id="landeshoheit"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** 'Territorial authority': a ruler's powers over his own territory within the Empire.
- **Explanation:** Confirmed in 1648, including the right to form alliances — but not against the emperor or Empire. It was not full sovereignty.
- **German:** Landeshoheit
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md`](../early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Landfrieden
<a id="landfrieden"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** 'Land peace': legal agreements to suppress feuds and violence.
- **Explanation:** 15th-century demands for such peace and law produced the 1495 Perpetual Public Peace.
- **German:** Landfrieden
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Landtag
<a id="landtag"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** Assembly of a territory's estates (nobles, towns) that voted on taxes; today a state parliament.
- **Explanation:** In early modern principalities the Landtag brought together nobles and towns, rarely peasants. The same word now names the parliaments of Germany's federal states.
- **German:** Landtag
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Landvolk movement
<a id="landvolk-movement"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** late 1920s
- **Short:** Farmers' protest movement in Schleswig-Holstein during the agrarian crisis.
- **Explanation:** Its protests preceded the Nazi breakthrough in the Protestant countryside.
- **German:** Landvolkbewegung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Landwehr
<a id="landwehr"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** from 1813
- **Short:** Prussian militia of 1813 made up of men not in the regular army.
- **Explanation:** It showed more genuine mobilisation in Prussia than in southern Germany.
- **German:** Landwehr
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Late development
<a id="late-development"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** late development
- **Short:** Gerschenkron's idea that latecomer countries industrialise differently.
- **Explanation:** Wehler linked Germany's rapid late industrialisation to social tensions.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### late industrialisation
<a id="late-industrialisation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Short:** Alexander Gerschenkron's idea that latecomer economies industrialise with bigger roles for banks and states.
- **Explanation:** Germany industrialised after Britain; Gerschenkron argued that big banks and the state helped supply what latecomers lacked. The KB lists this among debates on German economic history.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/industrialization.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Latin Christianity
<a id="latin-christianity"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Western Christianity using Latin and led by the bishop of Rome (the pope).
- **Explanation:** Post-Roman kingdoms like that of the Franks combined Roman administration, Latin Christianity and warrior elites. It gave medieval Germany its church, its written language and its link to Rome.
- **Main chapter:** [`ancient/01_germanic_societies.md`](../ancient/01_germanic_societies.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `medieval/03_medieval_society.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Lausanne Conference
<a id="lausanne-conference"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1932
- **Also written as:** Lausanne
- **Short:** 1932 international conference in Switzerland that effectively ended German reparations.
- **Explanation:** It achieved Brüning's goal of ending reparations — but too late to help him or the republic.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Law for Liberation from National Socialism and Militarism
<a id="law-for-liberation-from-national-socialism-and-militarism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1946
- **Short:** 1946 US-zone denazification law requiring every adult to fill in a questionnaire.
- **Explanation:** Passed on 5 March 1946 in the US zone, it required every adult to fill in a questionnaire. German tribunals then sorted people into five categories; only about 2.5% ended up in the two most serious ones.
- **German:** Befreiungsgesetz
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`](../twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Law for the Protection of German Blood and Honour
<a id="law-for-the-protection-of-german-blood-and-honour"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1935
- **Short:** Nuremberg Law of 1935 banning marriage and sexual relations between Jews and non-Jews.
- **Explanation:** It criminalised relationships across the Nazi racial line and was part of the legal exclusion of Jews.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Law for the Reconstruction of the Reich
<a id="law-for-the-reconstruction-of-the-reich"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** January 1934
- **Short:** 1934 law abolishing the German states as sovereign units.
- **Explanation:** It completed the centralisation of the Reich.
- **German:** Gesetz über den Neuaufbau des Reichs
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service
<a id="law-for-the-restoration-of-the-professional-civil-service"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 7 April 1933
- **Short:** 1933 law dismissing Jews and political opponents from public service.
- **Explanation:** It purged the civil service of Jewish and politically 'unreliable' officials.
- **German:** Gesetz zur Wiederherstellung des Berufsbeamtentums
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### lay piety
<a id="lay-piety"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** Lay piety
- **Short:** Religious devotion of non-clergy through confraternities, preaching and devotional books.
- **Explanation:** Lay people increasingly organised their own religious life, which prepared fertile ground for the Reformation of the 1520s.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Also in:** `medieval/03_medieval_society.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### LDPD
<a id="ldpd"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1945–1990
- **Short:** Liberal Democratic Party of Germany, a GDR bloc party.
- **Explanation:** Liberal party in the Soviet zone and GDR, subordinated to the SED as a 'bloc party'.
- **German:** Liberal-Demokratische Partei Deutschlands
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### leader principle
<a id="leader-principle"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** Führerprinzip · Führer principle
- **Short:** Nazi principle that the leader's will is absolute and all authority flows from him.
- **Explanation:** It made Hitler's authority absolute within the party after 1925 and, after 1933, meant that Hitler's will was law.
- **German:** Führerprinzip
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### leading role of the party
<a id="leading-role-of-the-party"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1968–1989
- **Also written as:** Leading role of the party · leading role
- **Short:** Constitutional claim that the SED must lead state and society.
- **Explanation:** Principle written into Article 1 of the GDR constitution in 1968 that guaranteed the SED's monopoly of power. The Volkskammer deleted it on 1 December 1989.
- **German:** führende Rolle der Partei
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/12_reunification.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### League of German Girls
<a id="league-of-german-girls"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Also written as:** BDM
- **Short:** Girls' branch of the Hitler Youth.
- **Explanation:** The BDM organised girls for sport, community service and indoctrination in the Nazi ideal of womanhood.
- **German:** Bund Deutscher Mädel
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### League of Nations
<a id="league-of-nations"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1920–1946
- **Short:** International organisation founded after WWI to keep peace.
- **Explanation:** Germany left it in 1933, confirmed by plebiscite.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### League of the Just
<a id="league-of-the-just"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1836–1847
- **Short:** Early socialist group of German craftsmen in Paris and London.
- **Explanation:** A secret society of German artisans abroad; it later became the Communist League, for which Marx and Engels wrote the Communist Manifesto.
- **German:** Bund der Gerechten
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md`](../nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Lebensraum
<a id="lebensraum"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** from 1901
- **Also written as:** living space
- **Short:** 'Living space': the Nazi goal of conquering and colonising land in Eastern Europe.
- **Explanation:** Hitler aimed to seize territory in the East, expel or kill its inhabitants and settle Germans there. This goal drove the war against Poland and the Soviet Union.
- **German:** Lebensraum
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md` · `twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md` · `twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md` · `twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Left Liberals
<a id="left-liberals"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Short:** Liberal parties critical of Bismarck, favouring parliamentary rights.
- **Explanation:** Part of the liberal-Protestant bourgeois milieu, alongside the National Liberals.
- **German:** Linksliberale
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### legality tactic
<a id="legality-tactic"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Legality tactic
- **Short:** Hitler's post-1923 strategy of winning power through elections, not a coup.
- **Explanation:** He pursued power legally while signalling that violence would follow — 'heads will roll', he told a court in 1930.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### legibus solutus
<a id="legibus-solutus"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Latin: 'released from the laws' — the claim that a ruler stands above the law.
- **Explanation:** The formal claim of absolutism; in practice German princes were bound by estates and imperial courts.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### legion
<a id="legion"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** legions · Legions
- **Short:** Large unit of Roman citizen soldiers, several thousand strong.
- **Explanation:** The Rhine legions were among Rome's largest concentrations of soldiers and could make emperors. Three legions (XVII, XVIII, XIX) were destroyed in the Varus battle of 9 CE.
- **Main chapter:** [`ancient/02_roman_frontier.md`](../ancient/02_roman_frontier.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Lehnswesen
<a id="lehnswesen"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** fief system
- **Short:** Fief system: grants of land or rights to vassals for loyalty and service.
- **Explanation:** The German term historians prefer to vague 'feudalism'. Fiefs became hereditary and princes treated them as property.
- **German:** Lehnswesen
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Also in:** `medieval/03_medieval_society.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Leibherrschaft
<a id="leibherrschaft"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** Personal lordship
- **Short:** Personal lordship: rights over a person's body — the core of serfdom.
- **Explanation:** The legal basis of serfdom (Leibeigenschaft).
- **German:** Leibherrschaft
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/03_medieval_society.md`](../medieval/03_medieval_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Leihezwang
<a id="leihezwang"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** Supposed 'obligation to re-grant': forced kings to re-grant forfeited princely fiefs.
- **Explanation:** Werner Goez showed there was no binding rule; kings re-granted because of princely pressure.
- **German:** Leihezwang
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: Mitteis vs Goez.

### Leipzig Central Institute for Youth Research
<a id="leipzig-central-institute-for-youth-research"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1966–1990
- **Short:** GDR institute whose secret surveys tracked young people's attitudes.
- **Explanation:** Research body whose confidential polls showed declining identification with the state among young East Germans in the 1980s.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Leipzig Reichswehr trial
<a id="leipzig-reichswehr-trial"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1930
- **Short:** 1930 trial of army officers accused of Nazi activity, where Hitler testified.
- **Explanation:** He swore to pursue power legally while announcing that 'heads will roll'.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Leitkultur
<a id="leitkultur"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** 'Guiding culture': contested idea that immigrants should adopt a core German culture.
- **Explanation:** It is used in debates over integration, assimilation and multiculturalism about how far newcomers must adapt.
- **German:** Leitkultur
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/03_modern_society.md`](../contemporary/03_modern_society.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### LeMO
<a id="lemo"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** Lebendiges Museum Online: German history portal.
- **Explanation:** 'Lebendiges Museum Online' is a German history website run by the Deutsches Historisches Museum and the Haus der Geschichte; it is cited here for resistance figures and expulsion estimates.
- **German:** Lebendiges Museum Online
