### Press decrees of 1863
<a id="press-decrees-of-1863"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1863
- **Also written as:** press decrees
- **Short:** Prussian decrees restricting press freedom during the constitutional conflict.
- **Explanation:** They curbed liberal opposition newspapers while Bismarck governed without a budget.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Press Law
<a id="press-law"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1874
- **Short:** 1874 imperial law establishing press freedom, with exceptions.
- **Explanation:** It ended prior censorship across the Empire.
- **German:** Reichspressegesetz
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Price decontrol
<a id="price-decontrol"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** price decontrol
- **Short:** Lifting official price controls so prices are set by the market.
- **Explanation:** Ludwig Erhard lifted many price controls alongside the June 1948 currency reform; goods that had been hoarded reappeared and shops filled almost overnight.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`](../twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### priesthood of all believers
<a id="priesthood-of-all-believers"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Luther's teaching that every Christian has direct access to God without priestly mediation.
- **Explanation:** It promised congregations autonomy, but in practice Protestant church government was absorbed into princely administration.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### prince-abbot
<a id="prince-abbot"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Also written as:** prince-abbots
- **Short:** Abbot who also ruled a territory as a prince of the Empire.
- **Explanation:** Like prince-bishops, a sign of how church and secular lordship fused in the Empire.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### prince-bishop
<a id="prince-bishop"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Also written as:** prince-bishops · Prince-bishops · Fürstbischof · prince-bishoprics
- **Short:** Bishop who was also a territorial prince, ruling land, courts and armies.
- **Explanation:** Germany had more church principalities than anywhere else in Europe — a legacy of kings endowing bishoprics with royal rights. Bishops of Würzburg, Bamberg, Münster and Salzburg ruled as princes.
- **German:** Fürstbischof
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `early_modern/01_reformation.md` · `early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md` · `medieval/03_medieval_society.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### prince-electors
<a id="prince-electors"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Also written as:** electors · electoral college
- **Ambiguous forms:** electors (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** The seven princes entitled to elect the king (fixed by the Golden Bull of 1356).
- **Explanation:** Archbishops of Mainz, Cologne and Trier, the king of Bohemia, the count palatine of the Rhine, the duke of Saxony and the margrave of Brandenburg. Their monopoly of royal elections made them kingmakers.
- **German:** Kurfürsten
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Princes' revolt
<a id="princes-revolt"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1552
- **Short:** Uprising of Protestant princes, allied with France, against Charles V in 1552.
- **Explanation:** It forced the emperor to negotiate and led to the religious compromise of the Peace of Augsburg in 1555.
- **German:** Fürstenaufstand
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Principality
<a id="principality"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** principalities · principality
- **Short:** A small state ruled by a prince.
- **Explanation:** The German Confederation included many tiny principalities alongside large states.
- **German:** Fürstentum
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md`](../nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `early_modern/01_reformation.md` · `early_modern/03_territorial_states.md` · `early_modern/05_enlightenment.md` · `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md` · `medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md` · `nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### principes
<a id="principes"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Short:** Latin 'chiefs' or 'leading men': Roman term for Germanic leaders.
- **Explanation:** Roman writers called leading men among the Germanic-speaking peoples principes. Like the reges, they depended on lineage, military success and gifts to followers.
- **Main chapter:** [`ancient/01_germanic_societies.md`](../ancient/01_germanic_societies.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### printing
<a id="printing"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Printing with movable type, developed in Mainz around 1450.
- **Explanation:** It spread humanism and national sentiment, and made towns the first centres of the Reformation.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md` · `medieval/03_medieval_society.md` · `medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md` · `themes/german_identity.md` · `twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### prisoner buyouts
<a id="prisoner-buyouts"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** 1963–1989
- **Also written as:** bought free
- **Short:** West Germany's secret payments to the GDR to free political prisoners.
- **Explanation:** Practice (Häftlingsfreikauf) by which the Federal Republic paid about DM 3.4 billion to 'buy free' some 33,700 political prisoners in 1963–89, a key source of hard currency for the GDR.
- **German:** Häftlingsfreikauf
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### privy council
<a id="privy-council"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** Geheimer Rat
- **Short:** A prince's top advisory and governing council.
- **Explanation:** Alongside specialised boards for finance and war, it formed the core of territorial government.
- **German:** Geheimer Rat
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### productivity
<a id="productivity"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Short:** How much a worker produces in a given time.
- **Explanation:** Borchardt argued Weimar wages had risen above productivity, making the economy 'sick' even before the Depression; critics dispute his data.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `contemporary/01_post_reunification.md` · `twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md` · `twentieth_century/12_reunification.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Professors' parliament
<a id="professors-parliament"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** professors' parliament
- **Short:** Nickname for the 1848 Frankfurt Assembly, dominated by lawyers, officials and professors.
- **Explanation:** The label highlights its educated membership; the KB warns the revolution as a movement went far beyond this bourgeois parliament.
- **German:** Professorenparlament
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Progress Party
<a id="progress-party"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1861–1884
- **Short:** Left-liberal Prussian party that opposed Bismarck in the constitutional conflict.
- **Explanation:** It prioritised parliamentary rights over national unity.
- **German:** Deutsche Fortschrittspartei
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Progressive taxation
<a id="progressive-taxation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** progressive taxation
- **Short:** Taxes that take a higher share from higher incomes.
- **Explanation:** One of the practical demands of the SPD's Erfurt Programme (1891).
- **German:** progressive Besteuerung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Progressives
<a id="progressives"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1910–1918
- **Short:** Left-liberal party of the late empire, part of the 1917 Reichstag majority.
- **Explanation:** Together with the SPD and Centre, it backed the 1917 Peace Resolution and sat in the Interparty Committee, a forerunner of the Weimar coalition. Its successor was the DDP.
- **German:** Fortschrittliche Volkspartei
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Proletarian women's movement
<a id="proletarian-women-s-movement"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** proletarian women's movement
- **Short:** Socialist women's movement led by Clara Zetkin.
- **Explanation:** Linked to the SPD, which was the first major party to demand women's suffrage (1891).
- **German:** proletarische Frauenbewegung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Proletaroid
<a id="proletaroid"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** proletaroid
- **Short:** Impoverished artisans living like wage labourers though formally independent.
- **Explanation:** Historians' term for masters and journeymen squeezed by competition who sank into near-proletarian poverty before 1848.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md`](../nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### proportional representation
<a id="proportional-representation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** PR
- **Short:** Electoral system giving parties seats in proportion to their share of the vote.
- **Explanation:** Weimar used it with no minimum threshold, which fragmented parliament; but the key factors in the collapse were anti-democratic parties' success and decree rule.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### protected Jews
<a id="protected-jews"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Early modern Jews allowed to live in a territory by a paid letter of protection.
- **Explanation:** Their residence depended on rulers' privileges and special taxes, not on equal rights. Legal emancipation came only in 1869/71.
- **German:** Schutzjuden
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/minorities_and_migration.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Protection treaties
<a id="protection-treaties"></a>

- **Kind:** term · treaty/agreement
- **Short:** Treaties with local leaders used to claim colonial 'protection'.
- **Explanation:** Often misunderstood or coerced, they claimed consent while rule rested on force.
- **German:** Schutzverträge
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### protective custody
<a id="protective-custody"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Short:** Imprisonment without trial by police order, used against political opponents.
- **Explanation:** Used against Rosa Luxemburg in WWI, it became a main tool of Nazi terror: the Reichstag Fire Decree of 1933 gave it a legal basis, and perhaps 100,000 Germans were detained this way in 1933 alone (Evans).
- **German:** Schutzhaft
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Protective tariffs
<a id="protective-tariffs"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** protectionism · protective tariffs
- **Short:** Import taxes that shield domestic producers from foreign competition.
- **Explanation:** Argued for by Friedrich List. From 1879 the Empire protected agriculture and industry by tariffs — the 'marriage of iron and rye'.
- **German:** Schutzzölle
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md` · `nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Protectorate
<a id="protectorate"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** protectorates
- **Short:** Territory placed under another state's 'protection', in practice a colony.
- **Explanation:** Germany's colonies of 1884–85 were officially protectorates.
- **German:** Schutzgebiet
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Protestant church
<a id="protestant-church"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** Protestant churches
- **Short:** The Lutheran and Reformed churches, majority faith in East Germany in 1949.
- **Explanation:** Harassed by the SED in the 1950s, the Protestant church gained a protected niche through a 1978 state–church agreement, and its buildings sheltered opposition groups in the 1980s.
- **German:** Evangelische Kirche
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)

### Protestantism
<a id="protestantism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** Protestant
- **Ambiguous forms:** Protestant (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Branch of Western Christianity that broke from the papacy in the 16th-century Reformation.
- **Explanation:** Protestant authors later recast ancient Rome as the papacy and the Germani as free Protestants. The old Roman/non-Roman line roughly foreshadows Germany's later Catholic and Protestant areas.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Protestants
<a id="protestants"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** Protestant
- **Ambiguous forms:** Protestant (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Followers of the churches that broke with Rome; the name comes from a 1529 protest.
- **Explanation:** At the Diet of Speyer in 1529 the evangelical estates 'protested' against revoking religious concessions — hence the name. It covers Lutherans and Reformed (Calvinist) Christians.
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Also in:** `ancient/01_germanic_societies.md` · `contemporary/03_modern_society.md` · `early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md` · `early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md` · `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md` · `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Proto-industry
<a id="proto-industry"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Short:** Rural home-based manufacturing (e.g. linen weaving) before factories.
- **Explanation:** Families produced goods at home for merchants. Mechanised competition from British factories ruined many such workers, as with the Silesian weavers.
- **German:** Protoindustrie
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md`](../nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### proto-state
<a id="proto-state"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** An early, incomplete form of state.
- **Explanation:** Older historians saw Carolingian government as a proto-state; recent work stresses negotiation with local elites instead.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/01_carolingian_world.md`](../medieval/01_carolingian_world.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Provisional Central Power
<a id="provisional-central-power"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1848–1849
- **Also written as:** Central Power
- **Short:** Temporary all-German government set up by the Frankfurt Assembly in 1848.
- **Explanation:** Headed by Archduke Johann as Imperial Regent, it had no army or revenue: the states kept soldiers and taxes, so it could not enforce anything.
- **German:** Provisorische Zentralgewalt
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Prussian constitutional conflict
<a id="prussian-constitutional-conflict"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1862–1866
- **Also written as:** Constitutional conflict · constitutional conflict
- **Short:** Clash in which Prussia's parliament refused army funds and Bismarck governed without a budget.
- **Explanation:** The liberal majority rejected the army reform; Bismarck kept collecting taxes anyway, using the 'gap theory'. Victory in 1866 brought an Indemnity Act that legalised it retroactively.
- **German:** Preußischer Verfassungskonflikt
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Prussian coup
<a id="prussian-coup"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 20 July 1932
- **Also written as:** Preußenschlag
- **Short:** Papen's removal of Prussia's SPD-led government by emergency decree in July 1932.
- **Explanation:** It eliminated the republic's most important bastion, including control of Prussia's police. Historian Ernst Piper judges that no other measure of Papen's government did more to prepare Hitler's seizure of power.
- **German:** Preußenschlag
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Prussian National Assembly
<a id="prussian-national-assembly"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1848
- **Short:** Elected Prussian parliament of 1848, dissolved by the king in December 1848.
- **Explanation:** It met in Berlin to draft a Prussian constitution; the king dissolved it and imposed his own constitution instead.
- **German:** Preußische Nationalversammlung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Prussian reforms
<a id="prussian-reforms"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1807–1819
- **Also written as:** Prussia's reforms
- **Short:** Reforms from above after Prussia's 1806 defeat: peasant liberation, town self-government, military and education reform.
- **Explanation:** Led by Stein, Hardenberg, Scharnhorst and Humboldt. They ended hereditary subjection, but on terms that strengthened large estates. A promised constitution was not granted until 1848/50.
- **German:** Preußische Reformen
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md` · `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Prussian state ministry
<a id="prussian-state-ministry"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** The Prussian government cabinet, closely linked to the imperial leadership.
- **Explanation:** Together with Kaiser, Chancellor and army leadership it held real power in the Empire.
- **German:** Preußisches Staatsministerium
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Prussian suffrage demonstrations
<a id="prussian-suffrage-demonstrations"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1910
- **Short:** 1910 mass protests against Prussia's unequal three-class vote.
- **Explanation:** They showed the gap between working-class numbers and political power.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Prussian virtues
<a id="prussian-virtues"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Prussian
- **Ambiguous forms:** Prussian (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Order, discipline, duty and obedience, traditionally associated with Prussia.
- **Explanation:** Values the GDR regime invoked despite its revolutionary claims — one of the continuities with older German traditions of state authority.
- **German:** preußische Tugenden
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### PSPP
<a id="pspp"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 2020
- **Short:** ECB bond-buying programme that Germany's Constitutional Court challenged in 2020.
- **Explanation:** The Public Sector Purchase Programme, in which the European Central Bank bought government bonds. In 2020 the Constitutional Court ruled against it, a rare clash with EU institutions.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md`](../contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md)
- **Also in:** `contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### public broadcasting
<a id="public-broadcasting"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** public broadcasters · Public broadcasting
- **Short:** Fee-funded radio and television (ARD, ZDF) run independently of the government.
- **Explanation:** Built on a 1950s Allied-inspired model to prevent state propaganda like that of the Nazi era. Parties sit on its boards, and populists contest it.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/03_modern_society.md`](../contemporary/03_modern_society.md)
- **Also in:** `01_RESEARCH_METHOD.md` · `contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### public sphere
<a id="public-sphere"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Öffentlichkeit · Public sphere
- **Short:** Space where private people debate public matters — in journals, clubs, coffee houses.
- **Explanation:** Habermas's concept. In the 18th century participation required literacy, money and leisure — a small minority. Critics say the idea idealises a bourgeois, male world.
- **German:** Öffentlichkeit
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/05_enlightenment.md`](../early_modern/05_enlightenment.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### public use of reason
<a id="public-use-of-reason"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Kant's term for free argument addressed to the reading public, as opposed to obedience in office.
- **Explanation:** Kant held the 'public use' must be free, while the 'private use' in one's office is bound to obey.
- **German:** öffentlicher Gebrauch der Vernunft
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/05_enlightenment.md`](../early_modern/05_enlightenment.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Pulpit paragraph
<a id="pulpit-paragraph"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1871
- **Also written as:** pulpit paragraph
- **Short:** 1871 law making political criticism from the pulpit a crime.
- **Explanation:** The first measure of the Kulturkampf against the Catholic clergy.
- **German:** Kanzelparagraph
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### purgatory
<a id="purgatory"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** In Catholic belief, a place of cleansing punishment after death before heaven.
- **Explanation:** Fear of purgatory shaped late-medieval piety — prayers, pilgrimages and donations aimed to shorten it.
- **German:** Fegefeuer
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/03_medieval_society.md`](../medieval/03_medieval_society.md)
- **Also in:** `early_modern/01_reformation.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

## R

### Racial hygiene
<a id="racial-hygiene"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** eugenics · racial hygiene
- **Short:** Eugenics: the idea of improving a population by controlling who may reproduce.
- **Explanation:** Widely accepted in medicine before 1933, it underpinned forced sterilisation and the murder of disabled people.
- **German:** Rassenhygiene
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Racial science
<a id="racial-science"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** racial science
- **Short:** Pseudoscience ranking human 'races'.
- **Explanation:** It survived the colonies into the Weimar and Nazi eras.
- **German:** Rassenkunde
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Also in:** `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### radical right
<a id="radical-right"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Also written as:** radical-right · far right
- **Short:** Nationalist, anti-immigration politics at the edge of or beyond democratic norms.
- **Explanation:** In Germany mainly represented by the AfD and smaller extremist groups. It radicalised after 2015 and became strongest in the East, while racist and right-wing violence has repeatedly struck since 1990.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md` · `contemporary/03_modern_society.md` · `themes/democracy.md` · `themes/german_identity.md` · `themes/nationalism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: causes of its strength in the East are debated.

### Radicals Decree
<a id="radicals-decree"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1972
- **Also written as:** Radikalenerlass
- **Short:** 1972 rule screening public-service applicants for loyalty to the constitution.
- **Explanation:** Agreement of federal and state governments that applicants for public jobs (including teachers) be checked for 'anti-constitutional' activity. About 1.4 million checks led to roughly 1,000–2,000 rejections (estimates). Critics see it as an example of restrictive policy in the Federal Republic.
- **German:** Radikalenerlass
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Rat
<a id="rat"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** Town council: the governing body of a medieval town.
- **Explanation:** Usually run by wealthy patrician families; after the 14th-century guild struggles, some towns shared seats with guild masters.
- **German:** Rat
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md` · `early_modern/03_territorial_states.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `themes/democracy.md` · `themes/political_power.md` · `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** Highlight only in the medieval town-council sense.

### Re-education
<a id="re-education"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** re-education
- **Short:** Allied programmes to teach Germans democracy.
- **Explanation:** Allied programmes to turn Germans towards democracy through democracy courses, American cultural centres (Amerika-Häuser) and a licensed press.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`](../twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reaction
<a id="reaction"></a>

- **Kind:** term · era/period
- **Dates:** 1850s
- **Also written as:** reaction
- **Short:** The 1850s period of restored monarchical rule after the 1848 revolution.
- **Explanation:** The Confederation was restored and the Basic Rights annulled, but Prussia kept a constitution and most feudal burdens stayed abolished.
- **German:** Reaktionszeit
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### reading revolution
<a id="reading-revolution"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 18th c.
- **Short:** Shift from re-reading a few religious texts to broad reading of novels and periodicals.
- **Explanation:** Rolf Engelsing's idea; it affected mainly the urban middle classes.
- **German:** Leserevolution
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/05_enlightenment.md`](../early_modern/05_enlightenment.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### reading societies
<a id="reading-societies"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** Clubs that pooled money to buy and discuss books and journals.
- **Explanation:** Hundreds existed by 1800, forming part of the new public sphere of the educated middle classes.
- **German:** Lesegesellschaften
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/05_enlightenment.md`](../early_modern/05_enlightenment.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### real existing socialism
<a id="real-existing-socialism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Real socialism
- **Short:** Official GDR term for socialism as actually practised, as opposed to theory.
- **Explanation:** It presented the existing party state as socialism's real form; critics used it ironically for the gap between promise and reality.
- **German:** real existierender Sozialismus
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/socialism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Real wages
<a id="real-wages"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Also written as:** real wages
- **Short:** Wages adjusted for prices: what pay can actually buy.
- **Explanation:** German real wages rose substantially from the 1880s to 1914.
- **German:** Reallöhne
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md` · `twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Realpolitik
<a id="realpolitik"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1853
- **Short:** Politics based on power and circumstances rather than principles.
- **Explanation:** Ludwig von Rochau coined the term in 1853; many liberals adopted it after 1848's failure.
- **German:** Realpolitik
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/nationalism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Realschule
<a id="realschule"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** Middle tier of German secondary school, more practical and less classical than the Gymnasium.
- **Explanation:** In the Kaiserreich's tiered system pupils were divided among Volksschule (elementary), Realschule and Gymnasium. The KB says such tracking has long reproduced social class.
- **German:** Realschule
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/education.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### rearmament
<a id="rearmament"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1930s
- **Short:** Rebuilding Germany's armed forces beyond Versailles limits.
- **Explanation:** Elites wanted it; under the Nazis it drove the economy, was hidden through Mefo bills, and pushed the regime towards war.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `themes/industrialization.md` · `themes/militarism.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md` · `twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### reason of state
<a id="reason-of-state"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Reason of state
- **Short:** Idea that a state's interests can justify a ruler's actions, beyond religious or moral rules.
- **Explanation:** It gained ground after the Thirty Years' War as weariness with religious war grew.
- **German:** Staatsräson
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md`](../early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md)
- **Also in:** `early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Recess of 1653
<a id="recess-of-1653"></a>

- **Kind:** term · treaty/agreement
- **Dates:** 1653
- **Short:** Brandenburg deal: estates granted army taxes; the elector confirmed nobles' rights over their peasants.
- **Explanation:** An early form of the crown–Junker bargain behind Prussian power.
- **German:** Landtagsrezess
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Rechtsstaat
<a id="rechtsstaat"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** A state bound by the rule of law, with independent courts.
- **Explanation:** The Empire was a genuine Rechtsstaat for most purposes, though opponents faced legal persecution.
- **German:** Rechtsstaat
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md` · `themes/political_power.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### reconstruction thesis
<a id="reconstruction-thesis"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Reconstruction thesis
- **Short:** Argument that the 'economic miracle' was mainly a catch-up to Germany's pre-war growth path.
- **Explanation:** Explanation, associated with Werner Abelshauser, that West Germany grew fast because it still had skilled labour and much of its industrial capacity, and was returning to its pre-war growth trend.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/industrialization.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### recruitment agreements
<a id="recruitment-agreements"></a>

- **Kind:** term · treaty/agreement
- **Dates:** 1955–1968
- **Also written as:** Recruitment agreements
- **Short:** Treaties by which West Germany recruited foreign 'guest workers' (1955–68).
- **Explanation:** Bilateral agreements (Anwerbeabkommen) with Italy (1955), Spain and Greece (1960), Turkey (1961), Morocco (1963), Portugal (1964), Tunisia (1965) and Yugoslavia (1968), to bring in workers during the labour shortage of the economic boom.
- **German:** Anwerbeabkommen
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### recruitment stop
<a id="recruitment-stop"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1973
- **Also written as:** Recruitment stop
- **Short:** West Germany's November 1973 halt to recruiting foreign workers, during the oil crisis.
- **Explanation:** The Anwerbestopp ended guest-worker recruitment. Paradoxically, because workers who left could no longer return, many stayed and brought their families — so the stop led to permanent settlement.
- **German:** Anwerbestopp
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Red Army
<a id="red-army"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** The army of the Soviet Union.
- **Explanation:** Its advance from late 1944 caused millions of Germans to flee; it took Berlin in 1945.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Red Army Faction
<a id="red-army-faction"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1970–1998
- **Also written as:** RAF
- **Ambiguous forms:** RAF (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Left-wing West German terrorist group, 1970–98, responsible for 34 murders.
- **Explanation:** Violent fringe that emerged from the radical left after 1968 (also called the Baader-Meinhof group). It attacked businessmen, officials and US bases; its 1970s campaign led to tough anti-terror laws.
- **German:** Rote Armee Fraktion
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Red Orchestra
<a id="red-orchestra"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Also written as:** Rote Kapelle
- **Short:** Berlin resistance networks with Soviet contacts; about 130 arrested, 49 executed.
- **Explanation:** Long dismissed in West Germany as mere 'spies', they are now recognised as part of the resistance.
- **German:** Rote Kapelle
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Redemption payments
<a id="redemption-payments"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** c. 1807–1850
- **Also written as:** redemption · redemption payments
- **Short:** Payments peasants made to buy themselves free of feudal dues owed to lords.
- **Explanation:** Peasant liberation in Germany was mostly not free: peasants compensated landlords in money or land. Redemption laws in the south were completed mainly after 1848.
- **German:** Ablösung
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reformation
<a id="reformation"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** 1517–c. 1555
- **Short:** Religious movement begun by Luther in 1517 that split Western Christianity into Catholic and Protestant churches.
- **Explanation:** Beyond theology, its biggest political effect in Germany was to put churches under the control of secular princes. It made religion a matter of territory and left the Empire permanently divided between confessions (denominations).
- **German:** Reformation
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `00_README.md` · `medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md` · `medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md` · `themes/continuity_and_change.md` · `themes/education.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### regalia
<a id="regalia"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Short:** A ruler's special income rights over mines, forests, salt and tolls.
- **Explanation:** One of the main sources of princely revenue alongside domains and taxes.
- **German:** Regalien
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### reges
<a id="reges"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Short:** Latin 'kings': Roman term for Germanic leaders whose power rested on lineage and war.
- **Explanation:** Roman writers called some Germanic-speaking leaders reges (kings). Their authority depended on noble descent, success in war and the ability to reward followers — not on a state.
- **Main chapter:** [`ancient/01_germanic_societies.md`](../ancient/01_germanic_societies.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Regulation Edict
<a id="regulation-edict"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1811
- **Short:** Hardenberg's 1811 law letting peasants own their land by ceding a third to a half.
- **Explanation:** An 1816 declaration excluded smaller holdings; many smallholders became landless labourers while nobles expanded their estates.
- **German:** Regulierungsedikt
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Reich
<a id="reich"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** German Reich
- **Short:** 'Empire' or 'realm': the German state as a whole, 1871–1945.
- **Explanation:** Used for the national level in the Empire (Reich budget, Reich Colonial Office), as opposed to the individual states.
- **German:** Reich
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** Also used for the medieval Holy Roman Empire and the German Empire of 1871–1918.

### Reich and State Citizenship Law
<a id="reich-and-state-citizenship-law"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1913
- **Short:** 1913 law defining German citizenship by descent (ius sanguinis), not by birthplace.
- **Explanation:** It let descendants of Germans abroad remain German while making it hard for immigrants to naturalise. Descent-based citizenship lasted until the reform of 2000.
- **German:** Reichs- und Staatsangehörigkeitsgesetz
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich Association Law
<a id="reich-association-law"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1908
- **Also written as:** Associations Law
- **Short:** 1908 law guaranteeing freedom of association, with restrictions on Polish in meetings.
- **Explanation:** It unified association law across the Empire but restricted the use of Polish in public meetings.
- **German:** Reichsvereinsgesetz
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Reich Chancellor
<a id="reich-chancellor"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Also written as:** Chancellor · chancellor
- **Ambiguous forms:** Chancellor · chancellor (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Head of the German government.
- **Explanation:** In the empire the Kaiser appointed the chancellor; from October 1918 he needed the Reichstag's confidence. In 1930–33 presidents appointed chancellors who governed by decree. In 1934 Hitler merged the office with the presidency as 'Führer and Reich Chancellor'.
- **German:** Reichskanzler
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich Citizenship Law
<a id="reich-citizenship-law"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1935
- **Short:** Part of the 1935 Nuremberg Laws: made Jews subjects without full citizen rights.
- **Explanation:** It limited full citizenship to people of 'German or related blood', turning Jews into second-class residents.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich Colonial Office
<a id="reich-colonial-office"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1907–1919
- **Short:** Imperial ministry for colonies from 1907.
- **Explanation:** Before 1907 a department of the Foreign Office ran colonies.
- **German:** Reichskolonialamt
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md`](../nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Reich Concordat
<a id="reich-concordat"></a>

- **Kind:** term · treaty/agreement
- **Dates:** 20 July 1933
- **Also written as:** Concordat
- **Ambiguous forms:** Concordat (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Treaty between Nazi Germany and the Vatican regulating the Catholic Church's position.
- **Explanation:** It gave the new regime international prestige.
- **German:** Reichskonkordat
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich Food Estate
<a id="reich-food-estate"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1933–1945
- **Short:** Nazi body controlling agriculture and food production.
- **Explanation:** One of the 'coordinated' organisations of the Nazi state.
- **German:** Reichsnährstand
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich Labour Service
<a id="reich-labour-service"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** RAD
- **Short:** Compulsory Nazi labour service for young people, introduced in 1935.
- **Explanation:** Young Germans had to do months of manual work, such as land reclamation, combined with political indoctrination and pre-military drill.
- **German:** Reichsarbeitsdienst
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich President
<a id="reich-president"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Dates:** 1919–1934
- **Also written as:** President
- **Ambiguous forms:** President (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Directly elected head of state of the Weimar Republic, with strong emergency powers.
- **Explanation:** Elected for seven years, he could rule by emergency decree (Article 48) and dissolve parliament (Article 25). Ebert used these powers to defend the republic; Hindenburg used them to govern without parliament from 1930 and appointed Hitler.
- **German:** Reichspräsident
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reich Railways
<a id="reich-railways"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** German state railway that transported deportees to ghettos and death camps.
- **Explanation:** The deportations depended on its trains and timetables, showing how the whole state took part in the genocide.
- **German:** Deutsche Reichsbahn
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reichsdeputationshauptschluss
<a id="reichsdeputationshauptschluss"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1803
- **Short:** 1803 decree abolishing nearly all church states and most imperial cities, giving them to secular princes.
- **Explanation:** It compensated princes for land lost to France; about five million people got new rulers. Winners were Baden, Württemberg, Bavaria and Prussia.
- **German:** Reichsdeputationshauptschluss
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Reichshofrat
<a id="reichshofrat"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** to 1806
- **Short:** Imperial Aulic Council: the Empire's second supreme court, in Vienna, close to the emperor.
- **Explanation:** Like the Reichskammergericht, it heard disputes between rulers and subjects, giving the Empire real legal weight after 1648.
- **German:** Reichshofrat
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)

- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reichskammergericht
<a id="reichskammergericht"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1495–1806
- **Also written as:** Imperial Chamber Court
- **Short:** Imperial Chamber Court: one of the Empire's two supreme courts, at Speyer, from 1693 Wetzlar.
- **Explanation:** Staffed jointly with the estates, it heard cases between rulers and subjects — even peasants could sue their prince. Strengthened in 1648, it handled tens of thousands of cases.
- **German:** Reichskammergericht
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)

- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reichsland
<a id="reichsland"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Dates:** 1871–1918
- **Short:** 'Imperial territory': Alsace-Lorraine's status as land held by the whole Empire.
- **Explanation:** It lacked full state status until 1911.
- **German:** Reichsland
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`

### Reichsmark
<a id="reichsmark"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** 1924–1948
- **Also written as:** Reichsmarks · RM
- **Short:** German currency 1924–48.
- **Explanation:** It was replaced in the Western zones by the Deutsche Mark in the currency reform of June 1948.
- **German:** Reichsmark

### Reichspatriotismus
<a id="reichspatriotismus"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** Imperial patriotism
- **Short:** Loyalty to the Holy Roman Empire as a protective legal order.
- **Explanation:** Strongest among smaller estates, imperial cities and jurists who relied on the Empire against larger neighbours.
- **German:** Reichspatriotismus
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)

### Reichsrat
<a id="reichsrat"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1919–1934
- **Short:** Weimar chamber representing the governments of the federal states.
- **Explanation:** It gave the states (Länder) a voice in national legislation.
- **German:** Reichsrat
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)

### Reichsschluss
<a id="reichsschluss"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Also written as:** Reichsschlüsse
- **Short:** A resolution of the Imperial Diet made binding by the emperor's ratification.
- **Explanation:** Decisions of the Diet's three colleges became imperial law only once the emperor ratified them as a Reichsschluss.
- **German:** Reichsschluss (pl. Reichsschlüsse)
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)

### Reichsstatthalter
<a id="reichsstatthalter"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Dates:** 1933–1945
- **Also written as:** Reich governors
- **Short:** Reich governors installed in 1933 to control the German states for Berlin.
- **Explanation:** They were a tool of 'coordination', ending the states' independence.
- **German:** Reichsstatthalter
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)

### Reichstag
<a id="reichstag"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1871–1945
- **Also written as:** reichstag
- **Short:** The national parliament of the North German Confederation, the Empire and Weimar.
- **Explanation:** In the Empire it was elected by universal male suffrage and approved laws and budgets, but could not appoint or dismiss the government. Its influence grew after 1890. The 1849 constitution had also planned a Reichstag.
- **German:** Reichstag
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reichstag fire
<a id="reichstag-fire"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 27 February 1933
- **Short:** The burning of the parliament building in Berlin in February 1933.
- **Explanation:** The Nazis used it as a pretext for the Reichstag Fire Decree, which abolished basic rights.
- **German:** Reichstagsbrand
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reichstag Fire Decree
<a id="reichstag-fire-decree"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 28 February 1933
- **Short:** Emergency decree suspending basic rights after the Reichstag fire.
- **Explanation:** It suspended freedom of the person, press and assembly, privacy of post and property rights, and gave a legal basis to 'protective custody' without trial. It was never repealed.
- **German:** Reichstagsbrandverordnung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`](../twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `themes/political_power.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reichsverweser
<a id="reichsverweser"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Dates:** 1848–1849
- **Also written as:** Imperial Regent
- **Short:** 'Imperial Regent': head of the 1848 provisional German government.
- **Explanation:** The title given to Archduke Johann of Austria, standing in for a future emperor.
- **German:** Reichsverweser
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Reichswehr
<a id="reichswehr"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1919–1935
- **Short:** The German army of the Weimar Republic, limited to 100,000 men by Versailles.
- **Explanation:** Conservative and semi-independent ('a state within the state') under Seeckt, its leaders were part of the power circle of 1930–33. It welcomed the murder of the SA leaders in 1934 and then swore a personal oath to Hitler.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/militarism.md` · `themes/political_power.md` · `twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md` · `twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### religious parity
<a id="religious-parity"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1648
- **Also written as:** parity
- **Short:** Equal legal standing of Catholics and Protestants in the Empire, established in 1648.
- **Explanation:** In the Diet, religious questions were decided by agreement between the confessional blocs, not by majority vote.
- **German:** Parität
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md`](../early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md` · `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `themes/political_power.md` · `themes/religion.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### reluctant hegemon
<a id="reluctant-hegemon"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Reluctant hegemon
- **Short:** Description of Germany as Europe's dominant power that is hesitant to lead.
- **Explanation:** Associated with William Paterson; a hegemon is a leading power. Whether Germany dominates Europe or merely depends on coalitions and rules is contested.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md`](../contemporary/04_germany_in_europe.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** CONTESTED: interpretations of German 'hegemony' differ.

### Remilitarisation of the Rhineland
<a id="remilitarisation-of-the-rhineland"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1936
- **Also written as:** remilitarisation of the Rhineland
- **Short:** Hitler's 1936 move of troops into the demilitarised Rhineland.
- **Explanation:** It broke the Versailles and Locarno treaties and went unopposed, emboldening Hitler.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Rentenmark
<a id="rentenmark"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** 1923
- **Short:** Temporary currency introduced in November 1923 to end the hyperinflation.
- **Explanation:** It stabilised the currency and opened the relatively stable years of 1924–29.
- **German:** Rentenmark
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### reparations
<a id="reparations"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** 1919–1932
- **Also written as:** Reparations
- **Short:** Payments the Allies required Germany to make for war damage after WWI.
- **Explanation:** Imposed at Versailles and restructured by the Dawes and Young Plans, they strained public finances. Brüning's austerity aimed partly at ending them, achieved at Lausanne in 1932 — too late.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `themes/industrialization.md` · `twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Replacement Army
<a id="replacement-army"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Short:** The Wehrmacht's home army, which trained recruits and replacements.
- **Explanation:** Stauffenberg was its chief of staff; after the 20 July plot, Himmler took command in 1944.
- **German:** Ersatzheer
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md`](../twentieth_century/07_world_war_two.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### republicans of reason
<a id="republicans-of-reason"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** Vernunftrepublikaner
- **Short:** People who supported the republic out of practical sense rather than conviction.
- **Explanation:** Among elites that were mostly monarchist or authoritarian, some — like Stresemann — accepted democracy as the reasonable option.
- **German:** Vernunftrepublikaner
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md`](../twentieth_century/02_weimar_republic.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Rerum Novarum
<a id="rerum-novarum"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Dates:** 1891
- **Short:** 1891 papal encyclical on workers' conditions and rights.
- **Explanation:** Pope Leo XIII's letter founded modern Catholic social teaching.
- **German:** Rerum Novarum
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### research university
<a id="research-university"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** University uniting teaching and original research, modelled on Humboldt's Berlin university of 1810.
- **Explanation:** Wilhelm von Humboldt's neohumanist reforms made research central to academic life. With the classical Gymnasium, it created an educated status group, the Bildungsbürgertum, tied to state service.
- **German:** Forschungsuniversität
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Also in:** `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md` · `themes/education.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reserve officer
<a id="reserve-officer"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Also written as:** reserve-officer
- **Short:** Part-time army officer rank, a coveted status for middle-class men.
- **Explanation:** It showed how bourgeois men adopted aristocratic military norms.
- **German:** Reserveoffizier
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/militarism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Reserve Police Battalion 101
<a id="reserve-police-battalion-101"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** Police Battalion 101
- **Short:** Unit of middle-aged Hamburg policemen that shot or deported about 83,000 Jews.
- **Explanation:** Christopher Browning showed its men were offered the chance not to shoot; only about 10–20% refused or evaded, and none was seriously punished.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Reserved rights
<a id="reserved-rights"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Dates:** 1870
- **Also written as:** reserved rights
- **Short:** Special rights kept by Bavaria and other southern states on joining the Empire.
- **Explanation:** Bavaria kept its own army in peacetime, post and railways.
- **German:** Reservatrechte
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Also in:** `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Resistenz
<a id="resistenz"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Broszat's term for widespread partial non-conformity that limited the regime's reach.
- **Explanation:** Distinct from active resistance aimed at overthrowing the regime: examples include Swing Youth, refusing the Hitler salute or listening to the BBC.
- **German:** Resistenz
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/05_everyday_life_under_nazism.md)
- **Also in:** `03_MENTAL_MODEL.md` · `twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### restitution
<a id="restitution"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Restitution
- **Short:** Returning property or paying compensation to victims of persecution.
- **Explanation:** Unlike West Germany, the GDR made no restitution to Jewish victims of Nazism.
- **German:** Restitution
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `early_modern/02_thirty_years_war.md` · `nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md` · `twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Restoration
<a id="restoration"></a>

- **Kind:** term · era/period
- **Dates:** 1815–1830
- **Also written as:** restoration
- **Short:** Period after 1815 when monarchs restored the pre-revolutionary order.
- **Explanation:** After Napoleon's fall, princes re-established dynastic legitimacy and suppressed liberal and national movements. The Biedermeier culture is associated with these years.
- **German:** Restauration
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md`](../nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md)
- **Also in:** `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `themes/authoritarianism.md` · `themes/education.md` · `themes/german_identity.md` · `twentieth_century/04_nazi_state.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### reunification
<a id="reunification"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1990
- **Also written as:** Reunification · Unification · unification
- **Ambiguous forms:** Unification · unification (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** The joining of East and West Germany on 3 October 1990.
- **Explanation:** Achieved through the GDR's accession to the Federal Republic under Article 23, after free elections, the currency union, the Unification Treaty and the Two-plus-Four Treaty. It was fast, peaceful and internationally accepted, but the economic transformation of the East was traumatic.
- **German:** Wiedervereinigung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/12_reunification.md`](../twentieth_century/12_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md` · `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md` · `contemporary/01_post_reunification.md` · `images/IMAGE_INDEX.md` · `twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md` · `twentieth_century/11_cold_war_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K
- **Note:** 'Unification' elsewhere in the KB can mean the 1871 founding of the German Empire.

### Revisionism
<a id="revisionism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** 1898–1903
- **Also written as:** Revisionism debate · revisionism
- **Short:** Bernstein's argument that socialism should be reached by reform, not revolution.
- **Explanation:** Formally rejected by the SPD but reflected in its reformist practice.
- **German:** Revisionismus
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/08_colonialism.md` · `themes/socialism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Revolution from above
<a id="revolution-from-above"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** revolution from above
- **Short:** Wehler's term for radical change imposed by the state and elites, not by the people.
- **Explanation:** Unification used revolutionary means — war, universal suffrage — to preserve monarchical and military power.
- **German:** Revolution von oben
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/05_unification.md`](../nineteenth_century/05_unification.md)
- **Also in:** `nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md` · `themes/authoritarianism.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### revolution of the common man
<a id="revolution-of-the-common-man"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Blickle's term for the Peasants' War as a broad revolt of peasants and townspeople.
- **Explanation:** It stresses that rebels combined religious justification with concrete economic and political demands.
- **German:** Revolution des gemeinen Mannes
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/01_reformation.md`](../early_modern/01_reformation.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Revolutionary Spring
<a id="revolutionary-spring"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Dates:** 2023
- **Short:** Christopher Clark's 2023 history of the 1848–49 revolutions across Europe.
- **Explanation:** Clark treats 1848 as a Europe-wide event with lasting institutional effects rather than a simple failure — part of the newer view that 1848 was a learning process. Older historians such as Veit Valentin and A.J.P. Taylor stressed failure.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md`](../nineteenth_century/04_1848_revolutions.md)
- **Also in:** `04_QUESTIONS_WORTH_EXPLORING.md`
- **Basis:** KB
- **Note:** CONTESTED: whether 1848 was a failure or a learning process is debated.

### rex Teutonicorum
<a id="rex-teutonicorum"></a>

- **Kind:** term · office/title
- **Short:** 'King of the Germans': papal title used to deny the emperor's universal claims.
- **Explanation:** In the Investiture Controversy papal polemic deliberately reduced the emperor to a mere king of the Germans.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md`](../medieval/02_holy_roman_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Rhenish League
<a id="rhenish-league"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1254
- **Short:** Alliance of Rhineland towns (1254).
- **Explanation:** An early example of towns banding together for defence and trade, since the distant king could not protect them.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Rhine crisis
<a id="rhine-crisis"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1840
- **Short:** 1840 French claim to the Rhine border that set off a wave of German patriotism.
- **Explanation:** It produced songs like 'Die Wacht am Rhein' and the 'Lied der Deutschen' — a rare moment of broad popular national feeling before 1848.
- **German:** Rheinkrise
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md`](../nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB

### Rhineland massacres
<a id="rhineland-massacres"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1096
- **Short:** Crusader massacres of the Jewish communities of Speyer, Worms and Mainz in 1096.
- **Explanation:** Crusader bands on their way east attacked the Jewish communities of the Rhineland cities. They were the first of the repeated waves of destruction of medieval German Jewry, followed by the Black Death pogroms of 1348–50.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Also in:** `02_MASTER_TIMELINE.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Richter debate
<a id="richter-debate"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 2021
- **Short:** 2021 controversy over Hedwig Richter's view of the Empire as democratically vital.
- **Explanation:** Critics like Conze and Herbert objected that she underplayed authoritarianism, nationalism and antisemitism.
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Basis:** KB
- **Note:** CONTESTED.

### Richtlinienkompetenz
<a id="richtlinienkompetenz"></a>

- **Kind:** term · German word
- **Short:** The Chancellor's constitutional right to set the guidelines of government policy.
- **Explanation:** Laid down in Art. 65 of the Basic Law. In practice coalition agreements and coalition committees heavily constrain it.
- **German:** Richtlinienkompetenz
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md`](../contemporary/02_modern_political_system.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### right to asylum
<a id="right-to-asylum"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Dates:** 1949–
- **Also written as:** Right to asylum · Art. 16
- **Short:** Constitutional right of politically persecuted people to protection in Germany.
- **Explanation:** The Basic Law's Article 16 (1949) granted it without conditions, a lesson of Nazi persecution. The 1993 asylum compromise (Art. 16a) restricted it, and EU asylum reform (GEAS, 2024–26) is changing it again.
- **German:** Asylrecht
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Righteous Among the Nations
<a id="righteous-among-the-nations"></a>

- **Kind:** term · other
- **Short:** Honour given by Yad Vashem to non-Jews who risked their lives to save Jews.
- **Explanation:** The honour Yad Vashem gives to non-Jews who risked their lives to save Jews. About 650 Germans have been recognised; the number changes over time.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Rintfleisch pogrom
<a id="rintfleisch-pogrom"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1298
- **Also written as:** Rintfleisch
- **Short:** Massacres of Jews in Franconia in 1298, named after their leader.
- **Explanation:** One of the medieval pogroms that destroyed Jewish communities in the German lands. Like other attacks, it was justified by false accusations — in this case that Jews had desecrated the communion bread (host desecration).
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md`](../medieval/04_cities_church_feudalism.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### ritual murder
<a id="ritual-murder"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** False accusation that Jews killed Christians for religious rituals (blood libel).
- **Explanation:** One of the accusations used to justify massacres.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/03_medieval_society.md`](../medieval/03_medieval_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Robotpatent
<a id="robotpatent"></a>

- **Kind:** term · law/decree
- **Short:** Habsburg decree limiting peasants' compulsory labour services (the 'robot') to lords.
- **Explanation:** Part of the Theresian and Josephine reforms that eased the burden on Habsburg peasants.
- **German:** Robotpatent
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md`](../early_modern/04_prussia_and_austria.md)

### Roman months
<a id="roman-months"></a>

- **Kind:** term · economic term
- **Dates:** from 1521
- **Short:** Unit in which the Holy Roman Empire's Diet voted military taxes on its members.
- **Explanation:** Named after the monthly cost of the army planned in 1521 to escort the emperor to Rome for coronation. Each territory's quota was fixed, and the Diet granted money for imperial defence as a number of 'Roman months' — one of the few taxes of the Empire as a whole.
- **German:** Römermonate
- **Main chapter:** [`early_modern/03_territorial_states.md`](../early_modern/03_territorial_states.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Romance
<a id="romance"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Also written as:** Romance-speaking
- **Short:** The languages that grew out of Latin, such as early French and Italian.
- **Explanation:** In the Frankish empire a Romance-speaking west met a Germanic-speaking east. The Strasbourg Oaths (842) record oaths in early Romance and early German, showing a language boundary within the Frankish elite — not two nations.
- **Main chapter:** [`medieval/01_carolingian_world.md`](../medieval/01_carolingian_world.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Romanticism
<a id="romanticism"></a>

- **Kind:** term · movement/ideology
- **Dates:** c. 1790s–1830s
- **Also written as:** Romantics · Romantic · romanticism
- **Ambiguous forms:** Romantic (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Cultural movement valuing feeling, nature, the past and national traditions.
- **Explanation:** It idealised the Middle Ages and the old Empire; after 1806 there was, in Burgdorf's words, a 'collective retreat into medieval romanticism'.
- **German:** Romantik

### Rosenburg files
<a id="rosenburg-files"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Dates:** 2016
- **Also written as:** Rosenburg
- **Short:** 2016 study of former Nazis in the West German Justice Ministry.
- **Explanation:** Named after the Bonn villa that housed the West German Justice Ministry. The 2016 study showed that in the 1950s–60s a majority of senior officials there were former Nazi party members.

### Rosenstraße protest
<a id="rosenstrasse-protest"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1943
- **Short:** 1943 street protest by non-Jewish wives in Berlin demanding release of their Jewish husbands.
- **Explanation:** In 1943 non-Jewish wives gathered in Berlin's Rosenstraße to demand the release of their arrested Jewish husbands. The men were released; historians debate whether the protest caused it.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)

### Rote Fahne
<a id="rote-fahne"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Short:** 'Red Flag': the Communist party newspaper, printed illegally 1933–35.
- **Explanation:** Underground copies were a main form of early communist resistance.

### Round Table
<a id="round-table"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1989–1990
- **Also written as:** Central Round Table · Round Tables
- **Short:** Negotiating forum of GDR government, SED, bloc parties and opposition, 1989–90.
- **Explanation:** From 7 December 1989 the Central Round Table, moderated by the churches, shared power with the Modrow government, oversaw the Stasi's dissolution and prepared free elections. It also drafted a new constitution that was never adopted.

<a id="royal-air-force"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Also written as:** RAF
- **Ambiguous forms:** RAF (see `ambiguous_forms.md`)
- **Short:** Britain's air force, which bombed German cities and dropped leaflets over Germany.
- **Explanation:** In 1943 the RAF air-dropped copies of the White Rose's last leaflet over Germany.

### Royal Frankish Annals
<a id="royal-frankish-annals"></a>

- **Kind:** term · document
- **Short:** Official year-by-year chronicle of the Carolingian court.
- **Explanation:** Source of the claim that 4,500 Saxons were executed at Verden in 782 — a figure historians question for textual reasons and because of the chronicle's propaganda purpose.

### RSHA
<a id="rsha"></a>

- **Kind:** term · institution
- **Dates:** 1939–1945
- **Also written as:** Reich Security Main Office
- **Short:** Reich Security Main Office: SS headquarters of the Gestapo, police and killing units.
- **Explanation:** Led by Heydrich and then Kaltenbrunner, it organised persecution, deportation and mass murder.
- **German:** Reichssicherheitshauptamt
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md`](../twentieth_century/06_holocaust_and_persecution.md)

### Rubble women
<a id="rubble-women"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Also written as:** Trümmerfrauen · Trümmerfrau · rubble women
- **Short:** Women who cleared bombed cities after the war; a partly mythologised image.
- **Explanation:** Much clearing was organised forced labour or done by paid firms; the image was later glorified.
- **German:** Trümmerfrauen
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md`](../twentieth_century/08_occupation_and_denazification.md)

### Ruhr miners' strike
<a id="ruhr-miners-strike"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1889
- **Short:** Mass strike of Ruhr coal miners in 1889 against which troops were used.
- **Explanation:** It showed the state siding with employers in labour disputes.
- **German:** Bergarbeiterstreik
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md`](../nineteenth_century/07_industrial_society.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Ruhr Poles
<a id="ruhr-poles"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Polish-speaking migrants from Prussia's east who worked in Ruhr mines.
- **Explanation:** A symbol of east-west internal migration in the Empire.
- **German:** Ruhrpolen
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md`](../nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/minorities_and_migration.md`

### rule of law
<a id="rule-of-law"></a>

- **Kind:** term · concept
- **Short:** Principle that the state is bound by law and citizens can challenge it in independent courts.
- **Explanation:** Rechtsstaat: a founding principle of the Basic Law, protected by the eternity clause. The GDR lacked independent courts, which is why it is sometimes called an 'Unrechtsstaat'.
- **German:** Rechtsstaat
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md`](../twentieth_century/09_west_germany.md)
- **Also in:** `05_REGIME_MATRIX.md` · `nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md` · `nineteenth_century/02_german_confederation.md` · `nineteenth_century/03_nationalism.md` · `nineteenth_century/06_german_empire.md` · `twentieth_century/10_east_germany.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### rural proletariat
<a id="rural-proletariat"></a>

- **Kind:** term · social group
- **Short:** Landless or near-landless farm labourers who lived by selling their labour.
- **Explanation:** In Prussia, peasants paid for their freedom from serfdom (1807–1850) with land cessions of a third to a half of their holdings. The landlords gained, and many peasants became landless labourers.
- **German:** Landproletariat
- **Main chapter:** [`nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md`](../nineteenth_century/01_napoleon.md)
- **Also in:** `themes/class.md`
- **Basis:** KB

### Russian invasion of Ukraine
<a id="russian-invasion-of-ukraine"></a>

- **Kind:** term · war/battle
- **Dates:** 2022–
- **Also written as:** full-scale invasion of Ukraine · 2022 Russian invasion
- **Short:** Russia's full-scale attack on Ukraine beginning 24 February 2022.
- **Explanation:** The invasion ended Germany's reliance on cheap Russian gas and its belief in peace through trade, triggering the 'Zeitenwende', rearmament and large German military aid to Ukraine.
- **Main chapter:** [`contemporary/01_post_reunification.md`](../contemporary/01_post_reunification.md)
- **Also in:** `00_FINAL_EXPLANATION.md`
- **Basis:** KB+K

### Russian Revolution
<a id="russian-revolution"></a>

- **Kind:** term · event
- **Dates:** 1917
- **Short:** The 1917 revolutions in Russia that ended in Bolshevik (communist) rule.
- **Explanation:** For Germans it served as either a model or a warning; fear of 'Bolshevism' shaped politics through the Weimar years.
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md`](../twentieth_century/01_world_war_one.md)
- **Basis:** KB+K

## S

### SA
<a id="sa"></a>

- **Kind:** term · party/organisation
- **Dates:** 1921–1945
- **Also written as:** stormtroopers
- **Short:** The Nazi party's paramilitary force ('Storm Detachment'), known as stormtroopers.
- **Explanation:** Several hundred thousand strong by 1932, it waged street violence and attracted unemployed young men. In 1933 it served as auxiliary police and terrorised opponents; its leaders were murdered in the 1934 Night of the Long Knives.
- **German:** Sturmabteilung
- **Main chapter:** [`twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md`](../twentieth_century/03_rise_of_nazism.md)
